## Document 044: 20261123_投資週報_AI基本面維持韌性，融資、利率與監管風險升溫

Source PDF: all report pdf/20261123_投資週報_AI基本面維持韌性，融資、利率與監管風險升溫.pdf
Report source: Yuanta (投資週報)
Date: 2026-11-23
Pages: 1
Extraction status: ok

### Page 1

任何擔保，如有錯漏或疏忽，本行及本行之任何董事或受僱人，毋須負任何法律責任。任何人因信賴此等資料而做出或改變投資決策，應審慎衡量本
身之需求及投資風險，自行承擔一切投資風險並自負盈虧，本行不負任何法律責任。本報告受中華民國著作權法保護為元大銀行所有，非經本行同
意，不得將本報告內容之一部或全部加以複製、轉載或散布。本報告所載述的意見本行可隨時予以更改或撤回，恕不另行通知。
金融市場投資週報                 2026 年 11 月 23 日
上週重點訊息
◼ 美國擴大 AI 晶片管制範圍，全球算力供應鏈面臨重組：美國 BIS 於 11/16 公布新規，將先進
晶片管制由實體出口延伸至第三國資料中心提供的遠端算力服務；向中國等受限地區提供合計
逾 1,200 萬 TPP (總處理效能) 算力的業者，出口特定晶片及設備須取得許可，並新增對東南
亞及巴西等地的出口許可要求。新規可能提高輝達、超微及海外資料中心合規成本，美系大型
雲端業者相對受惠，後續關注訂單轉移及全球 AI 算力部署變化。

◼ 輝達財報維持高速成長，SEC 會計調查疑慮重創市場信心：輝達 11/17 公布季度營收達 1,158
億美元，年增 103%，其中資料中心營收年增 110%至 1,076 億美元，顯示 AI 運算需求仍具
韌性。然而，11/20 Reuters 報導 SEC 正在檢視輝達可能涉及的會計問題，輝達當日股價重挫
14%，全週下跌約 23%。市場疑慮由 AI 投資回報延伸至財務透明度及企業治理，加劇半導體
產業賣壓，惟具體會計違規事項尚未獲確認。
◼ Fed 會議紀要釋出偏鷹訊號，市場重新評估升息風險：Fed 於 10 月 FOMC 會議中，以 10 比
2 票決議維持政策利率於 3.75%至 4.00%，兩名反對委員主張升息 1 碼；9 月整體及核心 PCE
通膨年增率估達 3.6%及 3.1%，顯示關稅、能源與 AI 投資需求仍帶來物價壓力。經濟與就業
雖具韌性，但通膨朝 2%目標回落的進展仍不穩定，市場須留意利率維持高檔甚至再升息的可
能，美債殖利率及高評價股票走勢將更受通膨與 Fed 政策影響。

市場展望
◼ 短線審慎，中期審慎偏正向，關注企業獲利與資金面變化：全球科技股修正已由評價調整擴及
企業治理、政策及資金面，市場風險偏好明顯下降，短期賣壓仍待消化。惟 AI 運算需求尚未
明顯反轉，主要 CSP 持續投入 AI 基礎建設，中長期成長動能仍具支撐。因此，美國科技股及
具 AI 供應鏈優勢的台、日市場，短期維持審慎，中期審慎偏正向。後續應聚焦企業獲利、訂
單及現金流，並關注 CSP 資本支出、Fed 政策與信用風險，配置以技術領先、財務穩健企業
為主。
◼ 市場避險情緒上升，中天期債券風險報酬相對均衡：Fed 政策與通膨不確定性仍可能造成利率
波動，但股市風險偏好下降推升美債避險需求，當前殖利率亦具配置吸引力。考量殖利率水準、
利率敏感度及信用品質，中天期美國公債與投資等級公司債風險報酬相對均衡，展望偏正向。
超長天期公債仍須留意財政赤字及期限溢價風險，投資級債整體信用品質穩健，惟須持續關注
AI 融資疑慮及信用利差擴大風險。

### Page 2

2  兆豐國際商業銀行 http://www.megabank.com.tw/
上週重要市場資訊
⚫ 美國 9 月通膨數據高於市場預期，CPI 月增 0.6%、年增 3.7%；核心 CPI 月增
0.3%、年增 2.6%。同月 PPI 月增 1.3%，創 2022 年 3 月以來最大增幅，年增
6.3%，顯示通膨壓力已逐步由能源價格擴散至更廣泛領域。數據公布後，市場一
度提高對聯準會 10 月升息的預期，但隨後多位官員釋出審慎訊號，使升息押注
降溫。聯準會理事鮑曼表示，決策前仍有時間觀察先前升息效果；舊金山聯準銀
行總裁戴莉則認為 10 月無立即升息之必要。而褐皮書顯示，美國就業市場維持
「低招聘、低裁員」格局，而高能源價格帶來的通膨壓力仍值得關注。
⚫ 中國 9 月通膨持續回升，CPI 年增 0.9%，創近三個月高點；PPI 年增 5.0%，創
逾四年最大漲幅，顯示通縮壓力進一步減輕。主要受能源與原物料價格上漲帶
動，且 9 月進出口表現優於預期，AI 產業鏈需求與貿易環境改善提供支撐；不

過，信貸增長仍仰賴政府發債與季底效應，內需與民間融資動能仍偏弱。
⚫ 日本銀行審議委員小枝淳子表示，不宜對升息過於保守，並認為政策利率仍有進
一步上調空間。總裁植田和男則強調維持 2%通膨目標的重要性。整體而言，日
本央行持續釋出偏鷹派訊號，有助支撐日圓表現。
⚫ 歐洲央行管委溫施表示，在能源價格波動下，為了控制通膨仍不排除進一步升息
可能，總裁拉加德則認為法國債務問題應透過財政政策解決，歐洲央行官員普遍
認為能源價格的潛在波動依然是推升通膨並使其居高不下的主要風險來源。。
⚫ 澳洲央行會議紀錄指出，能源價格上漲與需求韌性可能使通膨更具黏性，保留
進一步升息空間。然而 9 月就業數據意外疲弱，市場大幅下調升息預期，目前
預估 11 月維持利率不變的機率接近 90%。

### Page 3

3  兆豐國際商業銀行 http://www.megabank.com.tw/
美元兌新台幣匯率走勢
Oct/19/2026
＜上週走勢＞
上週台幣匯價高點為 31.890，低點在 32.520，以 32.338 作收，較前一周收盤價
貶值 4 角 6.3 分。
上週台幣走貶，周初，外資延續前周大幅匯出的走勢，雖逢高吸引出口商拋匯，
仍不敵外資匯出力道令台幣走貶；週二台股重挫近千點，外資大幅賣超台股 1653 億
並匯出，台幣接連跌破 32.000、32.100 及 32.200，總成交量 71.3 億，創歷史新高；
周三台股收漲，外資雙向進出，台幣收斂部分跌幅；週四，受美國通膨高於預期影
響，早盤台幣跳空開低，台股跌逾 2%，外資大幅賣超 3025 億，台幣跌破 31.400 關
卡，總成交量 91.2 億，再創歷史新高；週五外資持續匯出，台幣貶至周內低點 32.520，
終場在央行調節之下收在 32.338。
＜未來分析＞
預估本週台幣走勢偏弱。就國際市場而言，美國 CPI、PPI 雙雙高於預期，通

膨壓力仍在，儘管聯準會官員陸續發言表示不急於升息，淡化市場對於聯準會十月
升息預期，惟年底前聯準會仍有升息一碼空間，利率持穩於高位將給予國際美元支
撐；就台灣市場來看，市場對 AI 產業資本支出、融資成本及信用風險產生疑慮，若
賣壓延續，將壓抑市場風險偏好，增加外資調整台股部位及匯出的壓力，令台幣面
臨持續貶值的壓力，惟出口商拋匯將抑制台幣貶值速度，綜合以上，預估本週台幣
走勢偏弱。
＜預估本週＞ 美元兌台幣：32.100-32.700

### Page 4

4  兆豐國際商業銀行 http://www.megabank.com.tw/ 
美元兌人民幣匯率走勢  
Oct/19/2026 
＜上週走勢＞ 
上週人民幣震盪走升。上週人民幣中間價連續走升，週三中間價升至 6.7249 創
三年八個月新高，惟市場靜待美國公布通膨數據，交投謹慎，人民幣僅小幅走升；
週三晚間，美國公布 9 月 CPI 年增率 3.7%、核心 CPI 年增 2.6%，雙雙高於預期，
帶動美元指數升至年內高點 102.945、人民幣貶至周內低點 6.7005；然多位聯準會官
員發言表示聯準會不急於升息，令市場對於聯準會十月升息預期略有降溫，美元指
數自高點回落，激勵人民幣由貶轉升；臨近周末，聯準會主席華許於 IMF 會議發言
並未指引十月聯準會政策利率走向，再度淡化市場對於聯準會十月升息預期，國際
美元走勢偏弱，人民幣走升觸及年內高點 6.6825，終場境外人民幣收在 6.6850，週
線升值 91 點，境內人民幣則收在 6.6870，週線升值 60 點。

＜未來分析＞ 
     預估本週人民幣震盪偏升。就國際市場來看，美國仍面臨高通膨、年底可能將
再次升息，利差因素支撐美元指數於高檔震盪，限縮人民幣升值空間；就中國國內
市場來看，中間價持續偏升將帶動人民幣走升，加以出口商實質拋匯需求將支撐人
民幣表現；最後就技術面來看，USD/CNH 日線圖中，匯價維持在五日均線之下交
投，均線呈現空頭排列，加以 USD/CNH 有效跌破 6.6900 整數關卡，顯示 USD/CNH
空方勢力較盛，綜合以上，預估本週人民幣震盪偏升。 
＜預估本週＞ 美元兌人民幣：6.6500-6.7000

### Page 5

5  兆豐國際商業銀行 http://www.megabank.com.tw/ 
美元兌日圓匯率走勢  
         Oct/19/2026

＜上週走勢＞ 
    上週 USD/JPY 先上後下。週初，受油價攀升推升美國通膨與利率預期帶動，美
元指數溫和走升創 6 月底以來新高，推升 USD/JPY 突破 158.50；週間，美國公布 9
月 CPI 高於預期，帶動美元指數升至 2025 年 5 月以來高點，也使 USD/JPY 最高來
到 158.94，然逢高獲利了結賣壓出籠使匯價回落；臨近週末，USD/JPY 一度下探至
157.53，隨後逢低買盤進場收復部分跌幅，終場收在 157.99，週線下跌 13 點。 
＜未來分析＞ 
  預估本週 USD/JPY 區間震盪，主要原因如下：一、受美伊戰事推升汽柴油飆漲
與服務成本擴散影響，美國 9 月 CPI 及 PPI 月增漲幅均超出市場預期，加上初請失
業金人數連五週低於 20 萬人顯示勞動市場穩定，市場預期聯準會將於 12 月再次升
息，美日實質利差維持高檔，持續為 USD/JPY 提供下檔支撐。二、被視為鴿派的日
本經濟財政大臣城內實明確表態，貨幣政策應交由日本央行自行判斷，並宣告日本
已擺脫長期通縮、轉型為增長導向經濟體。此番言論降低政治干預日銀進一步升息
的疑慮，在貨幣政策正常化的腳步下，預計將限縮 USD/JPY 的上行空間。三、就技
術面而言，日線圖中，USD/JPY 匯價目前交投於月線及年線之間，各天期均線高度
糾結，多空雙方於 158 關卡附近拉鋸，在有效突破季線及年線反壓或跌破月線支撐
前，仍以區間震盪看待。綜合以上，預估 USD/JPY 本週呈現區間震盪的可能性較高。 
＜預估本週＞ 美元兌日圓：157.00-159.00

### Page 6

6  兆豐國際商業銀行 http://www.megabank.com.tw/ 
歐元兌美元匯率走勢  
         Oct/19/2026

＜上週走勢＞ 
    上週 EUR/USD 震盪走升。週初，持續受法國財政赤字與歐債利差擔憂拖累，
EUR/USD 震盪走弱；週間，因美國 9 月 CPI 高於預期，市場重新評估聯準會年底前
升息腳步，導致 EUR/USD 跳水最低來到 1.1112；臨近週末，儘管美國 PPI 與零售
銷售數據偏熱，市場優先反映美債殖利率回落與風險資產重新定價，美元多頭獲利
了結帶動 EUR/USD 谷底反彈，最高來到 1.1269，終場收在 1.1239，週線上漲 42 點。 
＜未來分析＞ 
  預估本週 EUR/USD 區間偏空震盪，主要原因如下：一、法國央行月度展望報
告指出，法國第三季 GDP 預估僅微幅成長 0.1%，印證近期高能源的成本已實質拖
累歐元區核心成員國增長。同時，歐洲央行總裁拉加德明確拒絕出手干預法債，要
求法國透過自身財政政策化解赤字困境；在明年法國大選前的財政隱憂與基本面疲
弱下，預計將令歐元上檔持續承壓。二、儘管歐洲央行管委溫施與總裁拉加德保留
12 月升息預期，加上德國基建與軍費支出為歐元提供部分下檔保護，但市場穩固預
期聯準會年底前將再升息一次。在美國經濟韌性與高利差優勢主導下，預計將限縮
歐元反彈空間。三、技術面而言，在日線圖中，EUR/USD 自低點反彈並站回五日線
之上，然而中長期均線下彎仍維持空頭排列，RSI 指標亦處於 50 中軸下方，顯示整
體空方趨勢尚未扭轉，反彈至月線附近恐面臨較大賣壓。綜合以上，預估 EUR/USD
本週呈現區間偏空震盪的可能性較高。 
＜預估本週＞ 歐元兌美元：1.1150-1.1300 
前週交易區間

一、 台幣貨幣市場部分，本週央行連續減發定存單使市場資金寬鬆，雖外資大舉匯
出，資金供給仍充裕，帶動短率朝區間低位移動。 
週五央行標售 2 年期定存單 250 億元，加權平均得標利率為 1.58%，低於市場
預期區間 1.60%-1.62%，較上期標售結果下跌 5.8 個基本點。 
週 五 隔 夜 拆 款 利 率 區 間 在 0.805%~0.830% ， 30 天 期 短 票 主 要 成 交 在
1.67%~1.68%，利率大致持平於上週。 
二、 5 年期指標券 A15108 週二搓合成交在 1.88%，後隨美債利率滑落，加上本週
2 年期公債標債結果提振市場持券信心，帶動 10 年期指標券 A15109 週四向
下成交在 2.02%~2.025%。 
在銀行去化短期資金及補充 LCR 之需求下，週三 2 年期公債得標利率為 1.65%，
落在市場預估區間下緣，投標倍數回升至 2.86 倍，銀行業得標比重上升至 88%。
股市承壓帶動避險需求，美債利率大幅回檔，促使台債交投偏空氛圍轉趨和
緩，惟在央行維持緊縮貨幣政策下，買盤積極追價的意願不強，預期短線台債
殖利率呈區間格局的可能性較高，關注下週 10 年券標售結果，預估 5 年券區
間為 1.85%~1.90%，10 年券區間為 1.98%~2.03%。

美國 10 月財政赤字大幅擴張、房市買氣探底，通膨壓力仍具韌性 
經濟 
⚫ 美國 2027 財年首月財政赤字達 4,040.6 億美元，年增 42.1%。當月總收
入 3,689.4 億美元（年減 8.8%），總支出 7,730 億美元（年增 12.2%），
截至 10 月底公債總額升至 40.52 兆美元，財政壓力顯著加劇。 
⚫ 受房貸利率攀升至 7.33%影響，2026 年 10 月美國成屋銷售年率跌至 375
萬戶的 16 年新低。目前房價中位數微升至 41.67 萬美元，待售庫存增
至 159 萬戶，房市呈現「量縮、價微揚、庫存增」的態勢。考量目前就
業市場仍持續成長，一旦利率開始回落，預期將能有效釋放市場積壓的
買氣。 
⚫ 10 月美國消費者物價指數(CPI)月增 0.4%，低於上月 0.6%，惟高於市場
預期；核心 CPI 月增 0.3%持平。住房、食品及能源價格續升，其中住
房貢獻逾三成，顯示通膨壓力仍存；惟受 2025 年 10 月政府停擺影響，
10 月年增率未發布。 
⚫ 10 月美國 PPI 月增 0.3%，較上月 1.4%明顯放緩，但年增率由 6.3%上
升至 6.5%，創 2022 年 11 月以來新高。能源與服務價格漲勢降溫，惟
食品、非能源商品及貨運價格續揚，顯示生產端通膨壓力仍高，後續須
關注成本轉嫁及對 Fed 利率政策之影響。 
重要 
事件 
⚫ 2026 年 APEC 峰會將於 11 月 18 日至 19 日在中國深圳舉行。會前中美
相繼延長部分關稅豁免及貿易限制暫停措施至 2027 年 1 月，中國暫停
24%對美加徵關稅，惟保留 10%稅率，美國則延長 178 項中國商品關稅
豁免，釋出經貿緩和訊號，有助降低供應鏈不確定性。惟雙方結構性分
歧仍存，後續須關注川習會談成果及貿易政策走向。 
⚫ 輝達創辦人黃仁勳因婚外情及涉國安爭議引發調查，於 11 月 12 日辭去
執行長及董事職務，結束 33 年掌舵生涯，由財務長克雷斯暫代執行長。
消息重挫輝達股價及市值，並引發市場對全球 AI 產業、供應鏈及公司
治理風險的高度關注。

一、 美國—10 月財政赤字大幅擴張、房市買氣探底，通膨壓力仍具韌性 
2026 年 10 月(2027 財年第 1 個月)聯邦政府財政赤字達 4,040.6 億美元，較去
年同期的 2,843.3 億美元大幅擴大 42.1% 。當月政府總收入為 3,689.4 億美元，年
減 8.8%，主因個人所得稅淨收入由去年同期的 2,169.5 億美元下滑至 1,863.8 億
美元，且 10 月關稅退稅高達 67.3 億美元(遠高於去年同期的 17.4 億美元)，大幅
抵銷了 259.5 億美元的關稅總收入，致使關稅淨收入雖較 9 月的 59.1 億美元回升
至 192.2 億美元，仍較去年同期的 313.5 億美元大減 38.7%。支出方面，10 月政
府總支出達 7,730.0 億美元，年增 12.2%，除因 11 月 1 日適逢非營業日，導致部
分現役與退役軍人薪資、退伍軍人福利及醫療保險等款項提前至 10 月撥付外，
醫療保險(1,700.5 億美元)、社會安全(1,426.9 億美元)及國防(1,288.8 億美元)等主
要項目均較去年同期成長；同時，10 月聯邦淨利息支出攀升至 1,030.8 億美元，
年增 13.4%。整體而言，在歲入下滑與福利、國防及利息支出擴張的雙重影響下，
美國新財年開局赤字顯著走高，截至 10 月底聯邦公債總額已進一步推升至 40.52
兆美元(其中公眾持有債務為 32.74 兆美元)，龐大債務規模與後續利息負擔仍將
是牽動美國財政穩健度的核心變數。 
2026 年 10 月美國成屋銷售經季節調整後年率降至 375 萬戶，較上月減少
5.5%，較去年同期下滑 8.8%，創下自 2010 年 8 月以來的最低水準，主因是 30 年
期固定房貸平均利率攀升至 7.33%(高於上月的 6.86%及去年同期的 6.25%)，使購
屋負擔加重，全美住房可負擔指數亦由去年同期的 107.3 降至 100.7。觀察區域表
現，10 月西部、南部及中西部銷量分別月減 9.7%、6.5%及 3.2%，僅東北部維持
持平，且四大地區銷量均較去年同期衰退。儘管買氣受抑，全美成屋售價中位數
仍年增 0.4%至 41.67 萬美元，寫下連續 40 個月年增紀錄；供給端則持續擴增，
待售庫存升至 159 萬戶，月增 1.3%、年增 4.6%，相當於 5.1 個月供給量(高於上
月的 4.7 個月與去年同期的 4.4 個月)。全美房地產經紀人協會(NAR)首席經濟學
家 Lawrence Yun 指出，在高利率衝擊下，10 月成屋市場呈現「量縮、價微揚、
庫存增」的格局，但全國 8,800 萬名屋主整體財務狀況依然穩健，不良資產交易
僅占 2%，考量目前就業市場仍持續成長，未來一旦房貸利率回落，市場可望釋

出可觀的積壓買氣。 
2026 年 10 月美國消費者物價指數(CPI)經季節調整後月增 0.4%，低於上月之
0.6%，惟高於市場預期的 0.3%；扣除食品與能源價格之核心 CPI 月增 0.3%，與
前月持平，並高於市場預期的 0.2%。在細項方面，住房指數月增 0.3%，貢獻整
體 CPI 月增幅逾三成，仍為推升通膨的重要因素；食品指數月增 0.5%，其中家庭
食品及外食價格分別上漲 0.4%及 0.5%；能源指數月增 0.7%，其中汽油價格及電
價分別上漲 1.0%及 0.9%，惟汽油漲幅較上月明顯放緩。核心項目方面，休閒娛
樂、醫療照護、汽車保險、家庭用品及教育等價格持續上揚，二手車及卡車價格
則下跌 1.2%，通訊價格亦小幅下降。另受 2025 年 10 月美國政府停擺影響，當月
未進行 CPI 調查並缺乏比較基期資料，美國勞工局未發布 2026 年 10 月整體 CPI
及多數主要指數之年增率。整體而言，10 月美國物價仍受住房、食品及能源價格
上漲帶動，核心 CPI 月增率則連續第三個月維持 0.3%，顯示服務類通膨壓力仍具
韌性，後續通膨走勢仍須持續觀察能源價格及住房成本變化。 
2026 年 10 月美國生產者物價指數(PPI)經季節調整後月增 0.3%，明顯低於上
月修正後之 1.4%，惟年增率由 6.3%升至 6.5%，創 2022 年 11 月以來最大漲幅，
顯示生產端價格壓力仍高。其中，最終需求商品價格月增 0.5%，低於上月之 2.5%，
逾六成漲幅來自不含食品及能源之商品價格上升，其中基本有機化學品價格上漲
3.9%；食品價格亦月增 1.3%，惟能源價格微降 0.1%，其中柴油價格下跌 7.9%。
另一方面，最終需求服務價格月增 0.2%，低於上月之 1.0%，主要受運輸及倉儲
服務價格上漲 2.0%帶動，其中貨運卡車運輸價格上漲 3.9%，貢獻服務價格漲幅
逾八成，惟貿易服務價格下降 0.2%，抵銷部分漲幅。剔除食品、能源及貿易服務
後之核心最終需求 PPI 月增 0.5%，與上月持平，年增率則由 5.0%小幅降至 4.9%。
整體而言，10 月美國 PPI 月增幅明顯放緩，主要反映能源價格回落及服務價格漲
勢降溫，惟食品、部分非能源商品及貨運價格仍持續上漲，加上整體 PPI 年增率
進一步攀升，顯示生產端通膨壓力仍高，後續須關注企業成本轉嫁情形、能源及
運輸價格變化，以及對消費者物價與聯準會利率政策之影響。 
2026 年亞太經濟合作組織(APEC)領袖峰會將於 11 月 18 日至 19 日在中國深
圳舉行。在峰會召開前及「川習三會」前夕，中美雙方相繼延長部分關稅豁免與
暫停措施，釋出緩和貿易緊張關係的訊號，並為後續經貿協商創造較有利條件。
中國宣布自 11 月 10 日起續暫停實施 24%的對美加徵關稅兩個月，並保留 10%稅

6

率作為後續談判籌碼，同時延長對美船舶特別港務費、出口管制及部分反制措施
的暫停期限；美國則將 178 項中國商品的 301 關稅豁免延長至 2027 年 1 月 9 日，
涵蓋工業產品、太陽能設備及電子零組件等項目，並同步延後對中國船舶港口費
及岸橋設備加徵關稅措施。此番互釋善意反映雙方均有意維持經貿關係穩定，降
低企業經營及供應鏈不確定性，並為 APEC 期間的高層對話創造有利條件；惟中
方仍保留部分關稅措施，顯示未完全放棄反制工具，後續發展仍將視雙方談判進
展而定。 
輝達(NVIDIA)創辦人黃仁勳近日在被爆出與疑涉中國情報背景的女子黃樂
(Alice Huang)有婚外情並於 11 月 8 日坦承對妻子不忠致歉後，因外部與董事會調
查持續延燒，且《紐約時報》與《路透社》接連揭露其與直屬下屬輝達醫療保健
副總裁金伯莉·鮑爾(Kimberly Powell)及另一名內部女員工也存在婚外情，最終於
11 月 12 日正式宣布辭去總裁、執行長與董事會職位，結束其掌舵輝達 33 年的傳
奇生涯，並由財務長克雷斯(Colette Kress)暫代執行長兵符，同時任命哈維·瓊斯
(Harvey C. Jones)為首任董事會主席。同時，美國總統川普於 11 月 9 日證實聯邦
調查局(FBI)已介入調查該起事件及潛在的反情報國安疑慮，並表示雖與黃仁勳通
話且相信其不知女方背景的說法，仍會交由情報部門進一步核實。黃仁勳閃辭的
消息隨即震撼全球科技與金融市場，導致輝達股價在 11 月 12 日開盤後盤中一度
重挫逾 14%、單日市值一度蒸發約 6,000 億美元，不僅重創高「含輝量」台股 ETF
表現，更引發外界對全球 AI 產業版圖及美中科技供應鏈穩定性的高度憂慮。儘
管黃仁勳矽谷盟友與部分員工近期發起連署與遊說，試圖推動他重返輝達，但美
國財政部長貝森特(Scott Bessent)已公開呼籲輝達領導層應進行「大幅改組」，使
後續接班與公司治理動向備受矚目。 
二、歐元區—勞動市場開始出現疲態 
 歐元區第三季 GDP 季增率二次估計值維持 0.4%不變，顯示在地緣政治不確
定性升高、能源價格再度上揚的環境下，整體景氣仍具支撐力；今年前三季經濟
成長率(年化率)則大約為 1.1%，大致持平去年水準。從需求面來看，政府支出與
民間消費為主要成長動能，其中民間消費受惠於較高的儲蓄水準及穩定的就業市
場，減輕能源價格上漲的衝擊，並且電動車、油電混合車需求持續增加，抵銷利
率升高及地緣政治波動對投資帶來的負面影響。

### Page 8

本報告係根據研究資料及各項可靠數據作成，兆豐銀行保留報告內容所有權利，未經本行許可，請勿引
用相關內容。

7

展望未來，從歐元區採購經理人指標(PMI)顯示第四季經濟仍可望延續溫和擴
張，在能源衝擊逐漸消退後，2027 年成長動能有望進一步增強。不過，第三季就
業呈現零成長，主要是德、法等核心國家就業疲弱，抵銷南歐國家就業增加的影
響，勞動市場可能逐漸走疲為未來復甦的隱憂。若就業狀況不如預期，恐進一步
抑制薪資表現，並削弱消費信心，進而影響經濟復甦。 
三、日本—經濟成長停滯，內外需動能轉弱 
 2026 年第三季經濟成長季增率持平，結束連續三季的經濟擴張，主要受通膨、
部分地區自然災害及全球需求疲弱影響，導致私人消費季減 0.3%，出口亦下滑
0.2%，加上進口反彈 2.5%，使淨貿易拖累 GDP 達 0.6 個百分點；惟企業投資季
增 1.6%，為近三季以來首次成長，對景氣形成一定支撐。整體而言，日本內外需
動能均有所轉弱，後續民間消費回溫及企業投資能否持續，將是觀察經濟能否恢
復成長的重要指標。 
四、中國—國際能源價格上漲抑制貿易順差擴張 
10 月出口年增率較 9 月續增 1.6 個百分點至 37.9%，出口總值為 4,123 億美
元；累計前十個月出口年增率上升為 22.7%，累積出口總值為 37,827 億美元。前
十個月出口主要成長項目(累積年增率)包含 IC(111.1%)、自動數據處理設備
(60.6%)、船舶(27.7%)、汽車(52.2%)，其中 IC 及自動數據處理設備出口加速成長，
為 10 月出口年增率加速上升的主要因素。 
10 月進口年增率較 9 月增 10.6 個百分點至 41.6%，進口總值為 3,071 億美
元；累計前十個月進口值成長 29%至 27,401 億美元。該月原油、成品油進口金額
大幅增加，為帶動進口加速成長的主要因素；IC、自動數據處理設備(包含伺服器
等)進口分別成長 65.1%、136.7%，呼應中國對高階 IC 的依賴，以及部分伺服器
關鍵組件有賴於進口滿足所需。進出口相抵後，前十個月貿易順差 10,426 億美
元，僅較去年同期增加 8.7%，增幅明顯低於出口，顯示出口擴張同時伴隨關鍵原
物料進口大幅增加，稀釋出口成長的加值效果，亦反映部分高附加價值產品依賴
國際供應鏈支持。此外，因貿易順差增幅有限，10 月外匯存底僅增 49 億美元至
約 3.41 兆美元。 
10 月生產者通膨(出廠價格)由 9 月的 5%升至 5.7%，主因原油價格居高不下，
燃料購進成本持續上漲，提高生產成本所致。當月石油和天然氣開採業出廠價格

本報告係根據研究資料及各項可靠數據作成，兆豐銀行保留報告內容所有權利，未經本行許可，請勿引
用相關內容。

8

年增率續揚 10.5 個百分點至 35%，並帶動燃料加工業出廠價格年增率上揚至
29.9%，此外，對於原油價格波動較為敏感的化學品、化纖出廠價格年增率加速升
至 16.4%、16%，顯示原油價格上漲的影響開始擴散至中下游產業。此外，由於
近期鋼鐵價格走弱，黑色金屬礦產開採出廠價格年減 6.4%，為主要拖累因素。 
10 月消費者通膨(CPI)、核心通膨(不含食品及能源)分別回落 0.2 個百分點為
0.7%、0.8%。儘管燃油通膨續增 5.3 個百分點至 20.2%，但食品、房租、汽車、
旅遊價格漲幅皆有放緩，其他商品及服務價格則大致平穩，使消費者通膨較前月
下滑。整體而言，雖然企業生產成本持續上升，但在民間消費乏力之下，企業仍
大多自行吸收成本，加上政府控制零售燃油及民生水電價格漲幅，故目前原油價
格高漲僅少數反映於消費端。惟考量能源成本傳導存在時間差，若油價維持高檔
或進一步上漲，未來通膨仍有進一步上升的風險。 
五、印度及東南亞—印度通膨超標；印尼、菲律賓第三季 GDP 分別達 5.5%及
2.1% 
(1)印度：10 月消費者物價指數（CPI）年增率進一步上揚至 6.09%，遠高於 9 月
的 5.54%，其中食品通膨加速上升至 8.5%，反映聖嬰現象導致季風降雨量低於預
期，影響農作物收成，同時核心通膨率（剔除汽油、柴油和貴金屬）從 9 月的 3.2%
上升至 3.8%，顯示潛在通膨持續走強並有所擴大。由於通膨率高於預期且突破央
行 6%的目標區間上限，預期印度央行 12 月的政策會議將有進一步升息的壓力。 
(2)印尼：2026 年第三季經濟成長率(GDP)年增 5.47%，私人消費成長 5.04%，主
要因政府採取措施，協助家庭抵禦通膨壓力和更高的借貸成本，惟受能源補貼負
擔加重導致財政空間收窄，政府支出大幅放緩至 8.95%，遠低於上季的 15.97%；
固定資產投資成長略放緩至 6.06%；另受石油和能源成本上漲影響，進口成長率
（7.31%）高於出口成長率（4.59%），致淨出口產生負貢獻，惟較上季對經濟的
拖累有所減緩。按產業別觀之，製造業(5.87%)、住宿業（11.22%）、運輸/倉儲業
（8.03%）和通訊業（7.13%）均有所成長，採礦業產出由負轉正 0.9%，抵銷農業
產出受聖嬰現象影響而大幅下降至 0.5%的影響。整體而言，第三季經濟成長優於
預期，惟在財政和貨幣政策收緊的背景下，國內需求可能進一步放緩。 
(3)菲律賓：2026 年第三季經濟成長率(GDP)年增 2.1%，為 2009 年第四季以來（不
計新冠疫情影響）最疲軟的經濟成長，低於政府先前已下調的全年 3.5%至 4.5%

用相關內容。

9

的目標，聖嬰現象令農業產量低迷和能源成本高漲持續抑制經濟活動。家庭消費
成長放緩至 2.4%，政府支出成長降至 6.8%，固定投資雖小幅復甦仍萎縮 4.4%，
所幸出口成長 8.6%、進口成長放緩至 7.7%，致淨出口呈正向貢獻。按產業別觀
之，工業成長加快至 5.4%，服務業小幅放緩至 9%，惟農業產出受氣候影響而衰
退 0.4%。整體而言，第三季經濟活動主要受氣候干擾，農業、服務產出下滑並影
響消費支出，固定投資在公共建設投資帶動下有好轉跡象，然而央行升息提高借
貸成本，對私人投資產構成壓力，隨極端天氣事件的影響逐漸消退、家庭在年底
假期前消費意願提升、以及政府加快基礎設施建設，將有助於第四季經濟加快復
甦。 
六、台灣—AI 商機持續發酵，出口動能強勁 
10 月出口達 967.6 億美元，年增 58.9%，創歷史新高，主要由 AI 及高效能運
算需求帶動，其中資通與視聽產品、電子零組件合計約占總出口 83%，顯示 AI 伺
服器、半導體及相關零組件已成為出口成長核心。同時，對美出口達 329.3 億美
元、年增 55.9%，東協亦大幅成長 83.8%，反映 AI 需求及全球供應鏈重組正帶動
出口市場結構改變。惟進口年增 75.1%，增幅高於出口，後續宜觀察進口增加是
否有效轉化為產能及出口，以及 AI 需求、高基期與全球科技業資本支出變化對
出口動能的影響。

## Document 096: 2027年度宏观经济展望：宏观向好，结构向新向优向绿

Source PDF: all report pdf/2027年度宏观经济展望：宏观向好，结构向新向优向绿.pdf
Report source: Unknown
Date: 未知日期
Pages: 47
Extraction status: ok

### Page 1

证券研究报告
2026年11月20日
行业：宏观
宏观向好，结构向新向优向绿
——2027年度宏观经济展望
分析师：陈彦利      SAC编号：S0870517070002
分析师：韦磊          SAC编号：S0870525030003

### Page 2

2
主要观点
u 海外经济表现分化，美国经济韧性，其中投资贡献上升，净出口拖累扩大。AI投资拉动了美国的私人投资，同时由于依赖
设备的进口，也加大了净出口的跌幅。美国2026年以来通胀水平明显高于2025年，受地缘政治因素带来油价上涨，仍是当
前美国通胀的主要症结。就业市场稳定向好，通胀压力下，美国开启加息。
u 国内经济外强内弱。进出口双强，投资消费偏弱。油价对于CPI的影响上升，工业品价格上下游均有所涨价。流动性充裕，
金融总量合理增长。社会综合融资成本低位运行，信贷结构优化。财政收入修复，支出偏慢，债务付息仍是负担。前三季
度4.6%的经济增速，有望顺利完成全年经济增速目标。
u 我们认为2027年的经济增速仍可能保持在4.5%-5%的区间。2027年作为十五五第二年，我们认为也有望延续26年的基调，改
革与发展并重，既要有空间，也要为后期夯实基础。2027年出口仍有望延续景气，出口动能继续向新向优，AI投资浪潮下
，高技术产品出口仍是重要支撑。投资方面预计仍受到房地产投资的拖累，制造业投资方面呈现结构分化，高技术产业投

资发展较快。消费方面，服务消费或更值得关注。适应居民消费结构升级，实施服务业扩能提质行动。
u 宏观向好，结构向新向优向绿。发展新质生产力是推动高质量发展的内在要求和重要着力点。
风险提示：地缘政治事件恶化；贸易摩擦加剧；AI投资进展超预期放缓；主要经济体货币政策超预期变化

3.4 美国加息压力犹存，但步调审慎
u 从美联储公布的最新点阵图来看，年内还有加息一次的可能，27年再加息一次后，随后将逐渐降息
u 我们认为地缘政治因素引发的通胀的高位致使美国存在加息的压力，但整体来看步调温和审慎，并非政策大幅反转，影响
将可控
图53 美国利率预测点阵图
资料来源：央视网，上海证券研究所

### Page 35

35
3.5 宏观总量向好，结构调整不断优化
u 前三季度4.6%的经济增速，且三季度经济增速有所反弹，有望顺利完成全年经济增速目标
u 十五五开局之年采用了区间目标是“为调结构、防风险、促改革留出空间，为后期更好发展打牢基础”，而2027年作为第二
年，我们认为也有望延续26年的基调，改革与发展并重，既要有空间，也要为后期夯实基础：
u 2027年GDP仍有望在4.5%-5%，宏观总量向好。
u 2027年出口仍有望延续景气，AI投资浪潮下，高技术产品出口仍是重要支撑。
u 投资方面预计仍受到房地产投资的拖累，制造业投资方面呈现结构分化，高技术产业投资发展较快。
u 消费方面，服务消费或更值得关注。适应居民消费结构升级，实施服务业扩能提质行动。

### Page 36

36
3.6 “五篇大文章”引领高质量发展
u 未来货币政策方向：总量合理增长，结构导向“五篇大文章”
u 保持金融总量合理增长，坚持实施好适度宽松的货币政策，增强货币政策前瞻性灵活性针对性，使社会融资规模、货币供
应量增长同经济增长和价格总水平预期目标相匹配
u 充分发挥货币信贷政策导向作用，用好各类结构性货币政策工具，扎实做好金融“五篇大文章”，加强对扩大内需、科技创
新、中小微企业、“六张网”建设等重点领域的金融支持
u 加强对实现高水平科技自立自强和建设科技强国的金融支持
u 为经济社会发展全面绿色转型和美丽中国建设提供高质量金融供给
u 完善多层次、广覆盖、可持续的普惠金融体系
u 健全应对人口老龄化的养老金融体系
u 推动数字金融高质量发展

2027 年營運重回成長

永豐觀點 
目前訂單能見度約一季，較先前有所改善。美洲客戶需求持續看增，預估明年成長
10%-20%。 
 投資評價與建議 
維持買進投資建議：主係考量(1) 新客戶訂單將逐漸貢獻營收，並持續精簡費用，
提升管理效率，2027 年獲利成長可期。(2) 主要營收區域歐洲及北美地區訂單狀
況皆轉好。評價上，以 2027 年稅後 EPS 5.04 元計算，目前本益比約 8 倍，明顯
低於過去五年本益比區間 10-17 倍，考量營運將重返成長，故給予 10 倍本益比
(原 11 倍)，維持買進的投資建議，下修目標價至 50 元。

2

全球前 15 大 POS 終端機製造業者：虹堡成立於 1993 年總部位於新北市新店區，
主要從事 POS 設備及相關產品之設計、開發與銷售，為台灣電子金融交易終端機
(POS)及 IC 晶片卡讀卡機製造商。主要營業項目為電子金融交易終端機(POS)及相
關產品之設計、開 發、銷售，這類終端機通過國際組織認證後經客戶在全球各地
通過當地收單機構驗證後即可用做當地商家收單使用。產品目前可支援各類型的支
付方式，包含非接觸卡片(contactless card)、晶片卡(chip card)、磁條卡(magnetic 
strip card)及新興的二維條碼及指紋辨識支付等。虹堡產品可概分為電子金融交易
終端機、個人金融應用產品、電子式收銀機及週邊設備等。國內主要競爭對手：振
樺電、樺漢、飛捷、伍豐等。 
記憶體價格飆漲，3Q26 獲利低於預期：在歐洲地區需求略有回穩下，虹堡 3Q26
營收 24.25 億元(+3.3%QoQ，+18.4%YoY)，受到記憶體價格上漲影響，毛利率
31.5%(+3pts QoQ，+4.6pts YoY)，因費用控管得宜，營利率 9.9%(+3.1pts QoQ，
+3.1pts YoY)，稅後淨利 1.5 億元(+25%QoQ，+8.1%YoY)，稅後 EPS 為 1.36 元，
低於預期的 1.52 元。 
物料狀況預期轉好，4Q26 持續成長：虹堡 10 月營收 6.83 億元(-27.4%MoM，-
1.8%YoY)，衰退係受原物料價格上漲及法國動亂影響，持累積第四季達成率
27.1%，低於預期，然管理層預期上下半年營收比重 45:55，預估在零組件價格穩
定後，訂單將穩定出貨。研究處預估 4Q26 營收 25.16 億元(+3.4%QoQ，
+8.6%YoY)，考量零組件漲價未能為轉轉嫁給客戶，毛利率 31.8%(+0.3pts QoQ，
-3.5pts YoY)，營利率 10%(+0.1pts QoQ，-2.7pts YoY)，稅後淨利 2.12 億元
(+41.6%QoQ，-27.5%YoY)，稅後 EPS 為 1.92 元。2026 年受記憶體價格上漲，
客戶下單轉為保守，預估 2026 年營收 89.1 億元(+13.3%YoY)，毛利率 30.9%(-
4pts YoY)，營業淨利 6.29 億元(-1%YoY)，稅後淨利 4.15 億元(-10.6%YoY)，稅
後 EPS 為 3.76 元。 
美洲地區客戶將開花結果，2027 年獲利重返成長：展望 2027 年，若以地區區分，
虹堡北美及南美客戶進展順利，許多專案已獲得客戶認證將開始出貨；歐洲地區，
法國新銀行訂單將持續出貨；亞太地區，因日本基期已高，2027 年預估微幅成長。
整體來說， 2027 年營收及獲利將重回成長的一年，然需關注記憶體價格對獲利及
訂單影響。研究部預估 2027 年營收 90.5 億元(+1.6%YoY)，毛利率 31.5%(+0.6pts 
YoY)，營業淨利 8.21 億元(+30.6%YoY)，稅後淨利 5.56 億元(+33.9%YoY)，稅
後 EPS 為 5.04 元。

### Page 2

the forecasts may be affected by incorrect assumptions or by known or unknown risks and uncertainties. The results ultimately achieved may differ substantially from these forecasts.
2
WESTPAC ECONOMICS
ECONOMIC INSIGHT
It was already our view that the RBA was likely done hiking
the cash rate after the November increase. Since then, our
conviction on that call has strengthened. Later today we
will publish the November Market Outlook with revised
forecasts. GDP growth, the labour market and housing

market have all been revised down in 2027.
Underlying inflation has been revised down slightly in the
second half of 2027, bringing the year-ended figure for
calendar 2027 more decisively inside the RBA’s 2–3% target
range than our previous forecast round. The unemployment
rate forecast has been revised up to peak above 5%. Based on
this revised forecast profile, an increase in the cash rate from
here would be unlikely and – we would argue – undesirable.

A range of considerations underlie this forecast revision. The
housing market has undershot our forecasts over the past
couple of months. Turnover and financing activity have been
coming in under our earlier expectations. Mortgage loan
inquiries have taken another leg down in October after the
September rate hike, after finding something of a level in prior
months, with the additional impact of the November rate hike
still to come.
We are also seeing early signs of a turn in the consumer.

Consumer income and spending have so far been quite
resilient to pressures from fuel prices and rising interest
rates. But our Westpac Card Tracker is starting to show that
discretionary services spending is turning down again in Q4,
joining the discretionary goods category that was already quite
weak. We would not over-emphasise this point, but it does
imply that the risks of further surprising resilience are fading.
Subsequent to the November Monetary Policy Board (MPB)

meeting, business survey data has shown weaker conditions
and a sharp decline in forward orders. They also show
further evidence that current inflation is mostly a non-
labour cost shock that is not being fully passed through to
consumer prices.
As we noted following the November MPB meeting, oil
and fuel prices are already materially lower than the track
assumed in the RBA’s forecasts. Dated Brent is nearly one-
fourth lower than on 28 October, the date the RBA’s forecasts

were finalised, and spot Brent is around 15% lower. This
significantly lowers the risk of a new wave of pass-through of
fuel costs to other prices, as well as easing consumer inflation
perceptions and expectations.
An RBA peak of greater conviction
Forecast changes
• A full update to our forecasts has been published in our
November Market Outlook.
Luci Ellis
Chief Economist, Westpac Group
In the media conference following the November meeting,
Governor Bullock flagged that it was a good time to “take

stock” of the policy tightening to date. Both we and the
market read this as a signal that the MPB would pause at the
December meeting. The 6:3 split vote at the meeting also
suggests that some external members are more attentive to
downside risks than the internal members.
A rate hike in February 2027 cannot be ruled out but it is
looking even less likely given signs in the data and surveys.
The MPB will at least discuss a hike in February. The staff and

senior officials remain sensitive to inflation risks and have a
downbeat view of supply capacity growth.
Given what we are seeing in the economy now, though,
a rate hike in 2027 would require either a material new
inflation shock that has not yet occurred, or would
constitute a policy error on the part of the RBA (something
that we will never forecast as a base case, knowing the talents
and capabilities of the staff and the Board). We concur with

the dissenting voters in the MPB that the downside risks need
more attention.
Policy is clearly restrictive, and the risk of overdoing the
inflation correction is growing. And by the time we get to
February, we think there will be more evidence that the
domestic economy is slowing materially, and the inflation
outlook with it.
“A deeper housing downturn and early signs of
cracks in the consumer sector point to an even
softer 2027."

WESTPAC ECONOMICS
THE WEEK THAT WAS
Cliff Notes: cost of living front and centre
the labour market while showing significant concern over
the strength and persistence of inflation, focusing on the
passthrough of energy and technology input inflation. His
conclusion for policy was clear: "if, as I expect, the monthly

rate of inflation improves in October, I am leaning toward
supporting an increase in our policy rate of 25 basis points
at the FOMC's December meeting"; but "if the October CPI
inflation data out later today are similar to the past two
monthly readings—with core CPI inflation around 0.3 percent
or even higher—then a stronger policy move may be needed".
Regional Fed Presidents Goolsbee, Logan and Hammack also
kept the focus on inflation risks and stated or implied the FOMC

needs to act. Market participants have responded to this week’s
updates, pricing a 25bp hike in for the December and January
meetings, and a cumulative 3.5 hikes by September. We believe
this pricing overstates the need for policy tightening given core
inflation ex shelter remains relatively benign at 2.4% on a six-
month annualised basis and the labour market is balanced; but
it will take time for price concerns to subside.
While we agree that the US economy is strong in aggregate,

US households are under pressure from the cost of living, and
their investment decision making is constrained by borrowing
costs. Highlighting this, US existing home sales fell 5.5% in
October after a downwardly revised 1.0% gain in September,
leaving the level of sales below the cycle lows of 2009 and
2010. Our November Market Outlook provide a full update on
our expectations for the US and the world.
In Australia, the Westpac-MI Consumer Sentiment Index

fell 2.7% to 78.2 in November, its weakest reading since
late-2022 and a level historically associated with periods of
severe economic stress. Renewed cost-of-living and interest
rate pressures were front and centre following another lift
in inflation, including sharply higher petrol prices, and the
RBA’s November rate increase. Mortgage holders are feeling
the squeeze most acutely, with sentiment across this group
falling to a record low. Consumers have become noticeably

more downbeat on the near-term economic outlook – the
relevant sub-index dropping 10.2% to be 22% below its
long-run average. Job-loss fears also continue to edge up.
Businesses are similarly troubled. The latest NAB business
survey showed confidence slipped further to –15 in October as
conditions held near their weakest level since the pandemic.
Of concern for the outlook, forward orders fell to –12, also
the weakest read since the pandemic, and capital expenditure

intentions deteriorated. Purchase costs continue to grow
materially faster than selling prices, further compressing
already tight margins.
Housing provides a clear example of how caution and tighter
financial conditions can jolt activity. The value of new dwelling
finance approvals plunged 15% in Q3, the largest quarterly
decline in the series’ history, taking approvals 25% lower year-
to-date. Investor lending bore the brunt of the adjustment,
falling 30% over the quarter as higher rates combined

with the housing tax changes announced in May. Owner-
occupier lending also weakened, however, with first-home
buyers proving particularly sensitive to the deterioration in
affordability and market conditions.
Taken together, the data points to a further loss of momentum
across the domestic economy, consistent with Westpac’s
expectation for subdued growth and concern over downside
activity risks.
Meanwhile, in the US, October’s headline CPI met our

expectations but at 0.4% was a touch above the consensus
view. Energy and food both contributed meaningfully to the
result, but core inflation was also marginally stronger than
forecast, +0.3%. Core goods inflation has accelerated over the
past four months to average a gain of 0.2% as businesses look
to pass tariffs, tech and energy input cost inflation through.
Shelter meanwhile continued to rise at an above-average pace,
gaining 0.3% in October.

The market impact of the October CPI report was accentuated
by hawkish remarks from Fed Governor Waller prior to the
release. He expressed confidence in economic activity and
Elliot Clarke, Head of International Economics
Ryan Wells, Economist
Illiana Jain, Economist

WESTPAC ECONOMICS
FOCUS ON NEW ZEALAND
Post election, where are we left?
This week markets and the economy needed to digest
the General Election results, as well as data on how the
economy is tracking through the final months of the year. A
centre-right coalition looks more likely to ultimately prevail,

although it may be a while before we know for sure and
longer again before a stable government emerges. Watch
this space as paths to victory for the centre-left remain.
Economic data are consistent with a still-strong external
sector but a weaker domestically-generated growth
impulse. Our upcoming Economic Overview next week will
examine these trends in more depth.
The real star of the show this week was the outcome of the
2026 General Election, which delivered an election night lead

to the centre-right coalition of the National, ACT and NZ First
parties. The preliminary result was a 67-57 seat margin in an
enlarged 131 seat parliament. Hence the centre-right won on
election night with a slender one-seat margin, given that 66
seats are required to form a majority. Financial markets and
the business sector generally cheered this outcome given their
preference for a centre-right government. However, the margins
are tight and there is the potential for the result to switch around

when the final vote count becomes available on November 27.
National leads Labour by narrow margins in two electorates
(Wairarapa and Hamilton East) and could lose those seats
when all is said and done. Also, special votes tend to swing
more to the left than the right historically and hence could
boost the centre-left bloc. The star performer of the election
was the Opportunity Party which debuted in parliament with
7 seats. The centre-left would require their support to get

over the line in all scenarios, whereas the centre-right might or
might not need them depending on how the special votes and
final electorate results play out. There seems a clearer path to
victory for the centre-right – especially if they can come to an
agreement with Opportunity – but clients should be wary of
complacency (which we note may be a risk).
A further complication is that the election left the incumbent
National Party in a much-weakened position as their coalition

partners picked up seats at their expense, and because their
much-reduced share of the party vote (29.2%) meant that
no National Party list MPs made it into parliament. Several
seasoned and senior government ministers needed a list seat
to make it into the new government including Finance Minister
Nicola Willis. Even if the final results confirm a centre-right
coalition win, there will be fierce debate within the coalition
on which party staffs key ministerial roles. ACT party leader

David Seymour is making a strong play for Finance Minister,
which Prime Minister Christopher Luxon is fiercely resisting.
There is the potential for tough and protracted coalition
negotiations to play out well into the Christmas period.
Indeed, I spoke on a post-election panel with an ex-National
Party senior minister this week who expressed the view that
it might not be until Waitangi Day (February 6) before a new
government is formed. Should anything like this play out then

the implications for economic and market uncertainty could
be significant. The initial relief rally in the New Zealand dollar
might prove premature in that scenario.
For interest rate markets, the main conclusion drawn is that as
a centre-left government seems a lower probability prospect,
then so also is the likelihood of a return to a ‘dual mandate’
for the RBNZ. Chief Economist Paul Conway made remarks
this week suggesting ongoing concern with inflation risks,

suggesting he is still in the hike camp at this stage. This has
seen markets firm up short-term OCR expectations such that a
25bp OCR increase in December is now seen as high probability
(85-90% currently). Bond markets cheered the prospect of
potentially tighter fiscal policy that could come with a centre-
right government. The spread between the 10-year government
bond and swap spread narrowed 4 basis points over the week
and the 10-year government bond yield differential to the US

fell to -49 basis points – the most negative since 2019.
Economic data showed further evidence of the two-speed
economy that is showing above-trend growth yet at the same
time feels quite unsatisfying to most voters. The low exchange
rate, generally robust global demand and tight supply
conditions in soft commodity markets have been delivering
consistently strong export returns for a couple of years now.
This week we saw another very strong GDT Pulse auction that

propelled whole milk powder futures higher – up some 20-25%
on year ago levels in USD terms. Significant upside risk for our
2026/27 Farmgate Milk Price forecast of $9.75/kgMS now exists,
which we will be delving into more deeply in our Economic
Overview to be released on Wednesday. The manufacturing
sector appears to remain in a solid state, with the manufacturing
PMI slipping a touch in October to 54.9 from 56.3 in September.
Short-term visitor arrivals have resumed their prior strong

uptrend, growing almost 3% for a second consecutive month
in September and almost 10% higher than a year earlier.
While arrivals from key European markets remain well below
year-earlier levels – likely reflecting the disruption to travel
through key Middle Eastern hubs along with higher jet fuel
prices – this has been more than offset by a very sharp lift in
arrivals from China. Indeed, Chinese arrivals in September
were almost two-thirds higher than the same month last year.

Michael Gordon
Senior Economist

5
WESTPAC ECONOMICS
And in the September quarter, arrivals from China surpassed
their pre-pandemic level for the first time. The number of
Australian citizens visiting New Zealand grew just under 6%yr
in September, but growth momentum from New Zealand’s
number one source market (a 30% share) does appear to have

slowed somewhat in recent months.
Less positive trends were evident in the more domestically
orientated areas of the economy. The most concrete indicator
of the NZ economy – concrete poured – dropped back a
touch in the September quarter, while consumer spending
in fell 0.7% in October. We have concerns on the quality
of the consumer spending data given that bank-sourced
data indicates a rise in consumer spending in October. But
nonetheless it’s probably reasonable to infer that higher fuel

costs in October crimped household budgets.
Housing market data for October were also week. House sales
fell and are down almost 22% y/y, while house prices fell in
October for the fifth consecutive month to be down almost
2% y/y. The weak performance of the housing market is likely
another factor weighing on consumer spending and residential
investment. That’s another topic we will be taking a look at in
next week’s Economic Overview.
Net migration flows for September confirm still modest

population growth. Arrivals of non-New Zealand citizens
continues to drift higher while departures declined a touch.
The net outflow of New Zealanders remains relatively high but
is declining, as signs of firmer employment growth emerge in
New Zealand at the same time as the Australian labour market
shows signs of weakening.

WESTPAC ECONOMICS
AUS: Oct Labour Force – Employment Change (000s)
Nov 19, Last: –39.3, Westpac f/c: 30
Market f/c: 27.5, Range: 9.9 to 42
Employment fell by 39.3k in September, well below expectations
for a 20k lift. Some of this weakness may reflect an unwind of
a possible residual seasonality stemming from recent changes

to the survey design, which saw August come in stronger.
Under the monthly noise, the labour market is loosening
gradually as jobs growth runs slower than population growth.
While some leading indicators point to near-term stability
– such as job ads – business surveys and detail within the
employment data point to some downside risk.
For October, we have pencilled in a lift in employment of +30k,
which would see employment growth hold steady on a three-

month average basis.
AUS: Nov Westpac Housing Sentiment (index)
Nov 17, Last: 85.3
The Westpac Housing Sentiment Index that appears in our
regular Housing Pulse reports will now be published on a
monthly basis a week after Westpac-Melbourne Institute
Consumer Sentiment is released. The Index is a composite
based on housing-related sentiment measures from the survey
and has proven to be an extremely useful predictor of turning
points in housing markets, both nationally and across states.

At 85.3, the Oct monthly Index read was holding near historic
lows, well below the long run avg of 100. The Nov update will
capture reactions to the RBA’s latest interest rate rise and the
deepening and broadening housing downturn. The release
will also include results from two new questions added to
the regular survey on consumers’ property buying and selling
intentions over the next twelve months.
AUS: Q3 Wage Price Index (%qtr)
Nov 18, Last: 0.8, Westpac f/c: 1.0

Market f/c: 1.0, Range: 0.9 to 1.1
The Wage Price Index (WPI) rose 0.8% in Q2, leaving annual
growth at 3.2%. Private sector wages continued to decelerate,
in line with a softer labour market, while public sector wages
provided a bit of an offset.
The Fair Work Commission's (FWC) 2026 Annual Wage Review
handed down a larger-than-expected 4.75% increase in award
wages. This will have a noticeable impact on the Q3 WPI, as the
wage increases stemming from the review are typically passed

on during the September quarter.
We have pencilled in a rise of 1.0% for Q3, lifting the annual
pace to 3.4%yr. Detail on wages growth by bargaining stream
will be closely watched for evidence of broader diffusion
beyond arrangements directly linked to the FWC's decision.

WESTPAC ECONOMICS
AUS: Oct Labour Force – Unemployment Rate (%)
Nov 19, Last: 4.7, Westpac f/c: 4.7
Market f/c: 4.7, Range: 4.6 to 4.8
Once again, the surprise in employment coincided with a surprise
in labour force participation, which fell 0.2ppts to 66.8% from
its near cycle high of 67.0% in August. This translated to a 26.2k

decline in the size of the labour force, which was less than the
drop in employment. As a result, the unemployment rate ticked
slightly higher in September, from 4.65% to 4.72% (although it
was steady at 4.7% to one decimal place), a new cycle high.
For October, we have the participation rate steady at 66.8%,
which together with the forecast rise in employment, would
see the unemployment rate also hold steady at 4.7%. Early
signs of rising retrenchments are a key signal to watch over the

coming months, especially if new hiring across other areas of
the economy starts to dry up.
NZ: Oct Selected Consumer Prices
Nov 17
Stats NZ’s monthly Selected Prices provides an update on
around half of the full CPI, including many of the more volatile
items. In terms of the big groups, we’re expecting a 0.1%
rise in overall food prices, with fresh produce prices typically
soft at this time of year. We also expect continued softness
in housing rents. However, the key focus will be on transport

costs. Petrol prices are expected to be up 11%. And we’ll be
watching to see if high global fuel prices are contributing to
further increases in airfares after recent large increases. There
is related uncertainty around holiday accommodation costs
(which drive much of the month-to-month volatility in overall
prices).
NZ: Q4 RBNZ Survey of Expectations (%yr)
Nov 17, Inflation 2yrs Ahead, Last: 2.34
With an easing in headline inflation in the September

quarter, we’re likely to see some easing in near-term inflation
expectations (one year ahead). However, longer term inflation
expectations, particularly the closely-watched two years
ahead measure, are likely to remain broadly steady. While
interest rates are moving higher, businesses are continuing to
report pressures on operating costs. In addition, despite their
recent easing, oil prices remain elevated. The only time longer-
term inflation expectations have moved materially below 2.3%

was in the mid-2010s. That followed an extended period when
inflation was running below 2%.

IN THIS ISSUE
Macro Strategy—Guide to El Niño: What It Is, Why It Matters, and What to Do
About It: As crude oil flirts with April’s highs, and the price of diesel sits comfortably
above $6 per gallon, disruption to the global energy complex has emerged as a major
theme of 2026. Add yet another variable to the equation: the current “El Niño” event,

on track to be one of the strongest on record. The upshot: more extreme weather
from droughts to heavy rains, warmer temperatures globally, lower crop production in
commodity-exporting economies, and higher prices across major agricultural products.
Importantly, these pressures won’t be equally distributed and will largely depend on the
magnitude and timing of weather events. Among the most vulnerable will be heavily
import-dependent countries in the Middle East, Africa and parts of Asia, while developed

economies that count food as a smaller share of consumer spending should be better
insulated. For investors, the prospect of a very strong El Niño only further reinforces our
conviction in several structural trends including a return to higher inflation and higher
interest rates, elevated commodity prices amid heightened geopolitical risk and supply
chain shortages, and greater investment in energy infrastructure and supply chain
resilience. In portfolios, we favor commodities as both an inflation hedge and diversifier.

Market View—Another Tale of Market Rotation: October offered another reminder
that headline index returns do not always tell the full story. The S&P 500 ended the month
slightly lower, but the more muted headline return masked meaningful shifts in market
leadership beneath the surface. At the center of the volatility was a sharp unwind in the
Artificial Intelligence (AI) trade, particularly across semiconductors and memory stocks. Yet
pressure on AI market leaders has not translated into a broad retreat from U.S. Equities.

Instead, capital has moved toward previously lagging areas of the market, with Value-
oriented, defensive and rate-sensitive stocks gaining ground. While it remains too early to
determine whether the shift will prove durable, October’s rotation may reflect less a retreat
from the AI theme and more a recalibration of valuations and expected returns as market
leadership broadens. For investors, the speed of the shift reinforces the importance of
diversification as the bull market enters a potentially more mature and differentiated phase.

Thought of the Week—Reorienting Around AI: From Corporate Plans to Construction
to Capital Goods: AI is reshaping the physical economy, and companies are reorienting
capacity and investment plans to meet surging demand for data centers, power and
infrastructure. Industrial firms are adding or reviving capacity, while data center construction
has increased more than 60% over the past year and is outpacing other major construction
categories. At the same time, robust capital goods orders and long lead times for inputs such

as generators, switchboards, chillers and transformers highlight both the strength of demand
and the supply constraints the buildout faces. Taking stock of these signposts, our view
is reinforced that the AI investment cycle remains in its middle innings, creating potential
opportunities for companies supplying the equipment and infrastructure behind the buildout.

MACRO STRATEGY
Guide to El Niño: What It Is, Why It Matters, and What to Do About It
Ariana Chiu, Assistant Vice President and Investment Strategist
As crude oil flirts with April’s highs, and the price of diesel sits comfortably above $6 per
gallon, disruption to the global energy complex has emerged as a major theme of 2026.
Add yet another variable to the equation: the current “El Niño” event, on track to be one

of the strongest on record. Below we answer frequently asked questions on El Niño,
the potential impact on the global economy and markets and, most importantly, how
investors can position portfolios accordingly.
What is El Niño, and what makes this time different? El Niño is the warm phase
of the “El Niño-Southern Oscillation,” a natural climate pattern occurring every two to
seven years. Simply put, El Niño refers to the warming of sea surface temperatures in

the eastern Pacific, historically leading to drier conditions in some areas (Southeast Asia,
Australia, Central America, southern Africa) and wetter conditions in others (southern
U.S., parts of Latin America) (Exhibit 1).
What makes this episode different? Should forecasts prove correct, this El Niño is on
track to be historic. The National Oceanic and Atmospheric Administration’s latest
outlook assigns at least an 83% probability that El Niño’s strength during October-

December will exceed that of any previous episode since 1950. Already, its impact has
been felt via wildfires in Indonesia, the weakest monsoon season in India since 2015,1
and severe flooding from Paraguay to Peru. The upshot: more extreme weather, hotter
temperatures globally, lower crop production in commodity-exporting economies, and
higher prices across major agricultural products.
Exhibit 1: El Niño's Impact Varies Globally.
Geography Typical rainfall pattern Specific impacts

South Asia Weaker summer monsoon risk Lower crop yields, water shortages, food inflation
Southeast Asia Drier Lower rice and palm oil production, wildfire risk, food inflation
United States Wetter in the south; warmer/drier in the north Southern flood risk; lower northern heating demand
Central America & Northern South America Drier Drought, lower crop yields, water shortages, food inflation, lower hydropower output
Southern Africa Drier Weaker maize harvests, food inflation, lower hydropower output

Western South America Wetter along the Pacific coast Flooding, infrastructure damage, disrupted fisheries
Southern South America Wetter Flooding and crop damage; potential yield gains in some areas
Australia Drier Drought, agricultural losses, elevated wildfire risk, food inflation
Source: National Oceanic and Atmospheric Administration, World Meteorological Organization, Food and Agriculture Organization, Australian Bureau of Meteorology, World Bank. Data as of

October 30, 2026.
How will El Niño impact the global economy and markets? Which regions will be
most affected? El Niño’s impact on the global economy and markets is likely to be felt
most via commodity price inflation. Alongside higher energy and fertilizer costs resulting
from war in Europe and the Middle East, El Niño could put further upward pressure on
commodities like wheat, sugar, corn, soy, cocoa and coffee as drought (or extreme rain)
weighs on crop yields and production. Latest estimates from the United Nations Food

and Agriculture Organization (FAO) suggest global food prices were up 5.8% year-over-
year (YoY) in September (Exhibit 2). Livestock is also likely to be challenged by higher
temperatures globally, while flooding has already threatened copper production in
Chile. Meanwhile, with hydropower accounting for more than half of the average annual
electricity generation in Brazil, Colombia, Peru and other Latin American countries,2
below-average rainfall in affected areas has huge implications for electricity generation.

As an example, Colombia’s natural gas demand nearly doubled during the 2023-2024 El
Niño event.2 Another key watchpoint will be whether drier conditions reduce water levels
1 India Meteorological Department, Bloomberg. Refers to rainfall between June and September as of September 30, 2026.
2 International Energy Agency, as of October 7, 2026
Investment Implications
We expect El Niño to place upward
pressure on agricultural goods
prices globally. We continue to

see commodities as a strategic
diversifier and hedge against inflation
in portfolios. The U.S. remains our
preferred region with companies in
Industrials and Materials as potential
beneficiaries. Meanwhile, economic
impacts to the emerging world will vary
by country but the broader Emerging
Market (EM) equity market could be
less exposed given its significant tilt
toward Technology.

### Page 3

3 of 8 November 2, 2026 – Capital Market Outlook RETURN TO FIRST PAGE
in the Panama Canal and other key shipping routes, particularly in light of the Strait of
Hormuz closure.
These pressures won’t be equally distributed and will heavily depend on this El Niño’s
severity. Among the more vulnerable to higher agricultural prices will be heavily import-
dependent countries in the Middle East, Africa, and parts of Asia. So too will economies
that count food as a larger share of consumer spending such as the Philippines (36%),

Indonesia (33%), Egypt (33%), India (28%), and Vietnam (26%) (Exhibit 3). Developed
economies like the U.S. (6%), Canada (10%), and Germany (11%) stand to be better
insulated from a food-related shock to headline inflation.
Within the U.S., some impacts could be positive with a potentially milder winter reducing
heating costs and wetter conditions in the South improving agricultural conditions. A recent
paper from the Federal Reserve Bank of San Francisco found that, in addition to putting

upward pressure on personal consumption expenditures (PCE) inflation, strong El Niño
episodes have been historically associated with a temporary boost to employment in the
following year via better construction conditions in the North and weather-event recovery
efforts.3 In California and parts of the Southwest, El Niño will be yet another stress test for
property insurers as flooding and landslide risks increase.
For more vulnerable developing economies, meanwhile, higher food and energy costs could

present a policy dilemma. On one hand, governments in vulnerable areas may move to
introduce fiscal relief to support households and boost growth. History points to plenty of
examples of this, such as Indonesia’s cash assistance in late 2023 or Thailand’s $1.8 billion
in loans to farmers in 2015. On the other hand, elevated inflation expectations and, in
some cases, weaker currencies could limit EM central banks’ room to ease monetary policy,
particularly with several major developed central banks aligned in hiking interest rates. Also

complicating matters: Global sovereign debt surpassed $110 trillion in Q2 2026, leaving
less fiscal space to absorb incoming shocks.4
How can investors position portfolios accordingly? In many ways, the prospect of a
very strong El Niño only reinforces structural trends we’ve identified as defining the coming
years: a return to higher inflation and higher interest rates; elevated commodity prices
amid heightened geopolitical risk and resource/supply shortages; and greater investment in

energy infrastructure and supply chain resilience.
Considering these themes, we continue to like commodities not just as a hedge against
higher inflation but also as a diversification tool in portfolios given their negative correlation
with Equities and bonds. That’s not to mention secular forces placing upward pressure on
commodity prices such as the resource-intensive AI buildout and broader protectionism. To
wit, the Bloomberg Commodity Index, which spans oil, gold, various metals and agricultural

goods has gained nearly 30% this year, handily outperforming the S&P 500 (+1%) and the
Bloomberg U.S. Aggregate Bond Index (-1%).
Within Equities, the U.S. is still our preferred region. While upside risk to inflation remains
a risk to watch going into 2027, food is a relatively small share of the consumer basket in
the U.S., and neither strong services spending nor capital investment should be materially
affected by El Niño. Companies within the Industrials and Materials sectors focused on

precision agriculture, water management and crop protection may be positioned to benefit.
Import-dependent EM economies remain particularly exposed, though impacts will vary
widely depending on the magnitude and timing of weather events. It’s worth noting too that
EM Equities remain heavily concentrated in East Asian markets with substantial exposure to
technology, leaving the broader index arguably less exposed to the economic ramifications
of El Niño than in past episodes. Indeed, even after recent weakness in semiconductor

names, Taiwan, Korea, and China together account for a near-70% of the broader MSCI
Emerging Markets Index.5 We maintain a slight overweight to the region in portfolios given
earnings strength but favor an active approach.

MARKET VIEW
Another Tale of Market Rotation
Kirsten Cabacungan, Vice President and Senior Investment Strategist
October offered another reminder that headline index returns do not always tell the
full story. The S&P 500 ended the month slightly lower, down almost 1%, but the more
muted headline return masked meaningful shifts in market leadership beneath the
surface. At the center of the volatility was a sharp unwind in the AI trade, particularly

across semiconductors and memory stocks, with the Philadelphia Semiconductor Index
experiencing a 19% intramonth drawdown. The dominant concerns in recent months
have centered on the sustainability of AI infrastructure spending and whether the scale
of investment will ultimately generate sufficient returns. Those only intensified amid a
sharp rise in bond yields after the 10-year U.S. Treasury yield spiked above 5.3% as of
October 5 for the first time since 2002, raising scrutiny over AI financing conditions and

whether higher borrowing costs could increase hurdle rates for continued investment
and slow the pace of capital spending.
Yet the pressure on AI market leaders has not translated into a broad retreat from U.S.
Equities. Instead, capital has moved toward previously lagging areas of the market,
with Value-oriented, defensive and rate-sensitive stocks gaining ground. Value defeated
Growth in October, with the Russell 1000 Value Index outperforming its Growth

counterpart by 10 percentage points, an outcome seen in only about 1% of months since
1979. The move extends Value's outperformance for the year, with the index up 23%
year-to-date compared with down almost 1% for Growth. Participation also improved.
The equal-weighted S&P 500 outperformed its market-cap-weighted counterpart by its
widest monthly gap since 2009 (Exhibit 4A), while the share of S&P 500 constituents
trading above their 200-day moving average rebounded to 62% from just 43% at the

end of September, and 76% of stocks outperformed the index for the month.
Exhibit 4: Market Leadership Has Been Shifting Beneath the Surface.

not possible to invest directly in an index.

The latest rotation is not the first attempt at a change in market leadership this year.
The more important question is what that shift represents: a fundamental reassessment
of the AI trade or another step toward broader, more mature market leadership?
Recent Q3 Technology earnings results have sharpened that debate. Investors are
increasingly looking beyond the scale of AI spending to companies’ ability to translate
that investment into durable growth and attractive returns. The result may be greater

dispersion not only between Growth and Value, but also among AI leaders as investors
become more discerning about which companies can justify the scale of their
investments.
Investment Implications
We continue to look for potential
missing opportunities and favor
diversification across and within asset
classes in a multi-asset portfolio.
In particular, we see potential
opportunities to broaden exposure
beyond AI leaders toward areas
where earnings, capital spending and

productivity trends are improving,
including Value, Small- and Mid-caps,
and select non-U.S. markets, particularly
EMs and Japan. As market leadership
evolves, maintaining diversified
exposures and periodically rebalancing
portfolios can help investors participate
in a broader set of possible return
drivers without becoming overly
dependent on any single theme.

### Page 5

5 of 8 November 2, 2026 – Capital Market Outlook RETURN TO FIRST PAGE
But greater selectivity does not necessarily signal the end of the AI investment cycle.
YoY growth rates may become harder to maintain after two years of rapid acceleration
as the comparison base rises, but absolute levels of AI capital spending remain elevated.
What matters more is whether spending translates into strong productivity and earnings
growth ahead and whether AI demand continues to support investment. October’s

volatility may therefore reflect less a retreat from the AI theme and more a recalibration
of valuations and expected returns, as other areas of the market begin to contribute
more meaningfully to market performance.
Several developments ahead will help determine whether that rotation has staying
power. One important test is whether broader participation can persist if pressure on AI
stocks continues. Continued strength outside the AI trade would support the case that

investors are reallocating capital rather than broadly reducing risk. The path of monetary
policy will also matter as investors assess where borrowing costs may be headed. The
Federal Reserve’s not-so-hawkish hold in October offered some reassurance, but the
path ahead remains dependent on the evolving balance between inflation and growth.
Any signs of resolution of geopolitical tensions remain an important swing factor
particularly as recent flashpoints in the Middle East have pressured energy prices higher

again. Results of the midterm elections should also provide greater visibility into the
policy landscape for the next few years. While the market does not need every clearing
event to resolve favorably, greater clarity around these uncertainties could help narrow
the range of potential outcomes facing investors.
For investors, October’s sudden change in leadership reinforces the importance of
diversification. Leadership can shift quickly, leaving portfolios that become overly

dependent on a single theme, sector or style particularly vulnerable when it does. We
remain constructive on U.S. Equities and believe that the market may be transitioning
more toward a mid-cycle phase in which returns become broader but also more
measured compared to the sharp acceleration earlier in the cycle. That argues not for
abandoning the market’s long-term winners, but for complementing them with exposure
to additional sources of returns across style, size, sector and region.

### Page 6

6 of 8 November 2, 2026 – Capital Market Outlook RETURN TO FIRST PAGE
THOUGHT OF THE WEEK
Reorienting around AI: From Corporate Plans to Construction to Capital Goods
Lauren Sanfilippo, Director and Senior Investment Strategist
For all the questions (and recent market weakness) hanging over the AI trade, from
monetization and cash burn to financing risk and overcapacity, a measurable aspect is
the buildout itself. Data center construction is lifting construction spending and capital

goods orders, while companies are adding and reviving capacity to meet AI-related
demand. Still, planning delays, access to power, labor scarcity, local politics and the cost
of capital remain meaningful constraints on the buildout’s pace or profitability.
Corporate Plans. Take Caterpillar as one example that has resumed production of a
10-megawatt natural gas turbine discontinued back in 2022. A 175-year old glassmaker,
Corning, is leaning into optical connectivity as AI clusters require faster connections of

optical fiber cabling between servers. Caterpillar and Corning are just two companies
among others pivoting to supply data centers.
Construction to Capital Goods. The AI reorientation is evident in construction
spending as well, where data center construction has outpaced every other major
residential and nonresidential category. Over the last year AI-related construction on data
centers has surged more than 60% while spending on residential, lodging, commercial

or office has declined notably (Exhibit 5A). Data center construction is running at an $85
billion (B) annualized rate this year, versus roughly $49B a year earlier, and now accounts
for 11% of private nonresidential construction, up from just 2% in 2022 according to
the Census Bureau. Similarly, demand for artificial intelligence business equipment and
transportation equipment drove an increase in durable goods orders in September on the
back of new orders for U.S.-manufactured capital goods, suggesting business spending

on equipment remained robust in Q3. Another factor, see Exhibit 5B, where critical
components such as generators, switchboards, chillers and transformers continue to
face long lead times that affect delivery timelines and ultimate profitability for new data
center development.
These signposts reinforce our view that we are only in the middle innings of the AI
investment cycle. From an investment perspective, huge demand for data centers, power
systems, networking infrastructure, specialized chips and other equipment continues

to strain supply, creating both bottlenecks and potential opportunities for companies
powering the buildout.

Index Definitions
Securities indexes assume reinvestment of all distributions and interest payments. Indexes are unmanaged and do not take into account fees or expenses. It is not possible to
invest directly in an index. Indexes are all based in U.S. dollars.
S&P 500/Market-cap Weighted Index is a market-capitalization-weighted index that is widely regarded as the best single gauge of large-cap U.S. equities. The index includes 500 leading

companies and covers approximately 80% of available market capitalization.
S&P 500 Equal-Weighted Index includes the same constituents as the capitalization weighted S&P 500, but each company in the S&P 500 EWI is allocated a fixed weight - or 0.2% of the
index total at each quarterly rebalance.
Bloomberg U.S. Aggregate Bond Index is a broad-based, market-capitalization-weighted benchmark that measures the performance of the U.S. investment-grade, U.S. dollar-denominated,

fixed-rate taxable bond market
Bloomberg Commodity Index is a broadly diversified financial benchmark that tracks the price movements of physical commodities through futures contracts.
MSCI Emerging Market Index captures large and mid cap representation across 24 Emerging Markets (EM) countries.
United Nations Food and Agriculture Food Price Index is a measure of the monthly change in international prices of a basket of food commodities.
Philadelphia Semiconductor Index is a market-cap-weighted index focusing on 30 leading semiconductor firms, offering insights into the tech market.

the companies or markets, as well as economic, political or social events in the U.S. or abroad. Small cap and mid cap companies pose special risks, including possible illiquidity and greater price
volatility than funds consisting of larger, more established companies. Investing in fixed-income securities may involve certain risks, including the credit quality of individual issuers, possible
prepayments, market or economic developments and yields and share price fluctuations due to changes in interest rates. When interest rates go up, bond prices typically drop, and vice versa.

Investments in high-yield bonds (sometimes referred to as “junk bonds”) offer the potential for high current income and attractive total return, but involves certain risks. Changes in economic
conditions or other circumstances may adversely affect a junk bond issuer’s ability to make principal and interest payments. Income from investing in municipal bonds is generally exempt
from Federal and state taxes for residents of the issuing state. While the interest income is tax-exempt, any capital gains distributed are taxable to the investor. Income for some investors

may be subject to the Federal Alternative Minimum Tax (AMT). Treasury bills are less volatile than longer-term fixed income securities and are guaranteed as to timely payment of principal and
interest by the U.S. government. Bonds are subject to interest rate, inflation and credit risks. Investments in foreign securities (including ADRs) involve special risks, including foreign currency
risk and the possibility of substantial volatility due to adverse political, economic or other developments. These risks are magnified for investments made in emerging markets. Investments in

a certain industry or sector may pose additional risk due to lack of diversification and sector concentration. There are special risks associated with an investment in commodities, such as gold,
including market price fluctuations, regulatory changes, interest rate changes, credit risk, economic changes and the impact of adverse political or financial factors.
Alternative investments are speculative and involve a high degree of risk.
Alternative investments are intended for qualified investors only. Alternative Investments such as derivatives, hedge funds, private credit, private equity funds, and funds of funds can result

in higher return potential but also higher loss potential. Changes in economic conditions or other circumstances may adversely affect your investments. Before you invest in alternative
investments, you should consider your overall financial situation, how much money you have to invest, your need for liquidity, and your tolerance for risk.

2
WESTPAC ECONOMICS
BULLETIN
The six-month annualised growth rate in the Westpac–
Melbourne Institute Leading Index, which indicates the
likely pace of economic activity relative to trend three to
nine months into the future, declined to –0.14% in October
from +0.06% in September.

The Leading Index weakened again in October after steadier
results through August-September. At –0.14% the latest six
month growth rate is back in negative, below-trend territory,
broadly similar to the soft reads around the start of the year.
The shift points to a return to below-trend growth momentum
in the first half of 2027. Westpac expects annual GDP growth
to slow back to 1.4% for calendar 2026, with a further
moderation to a sluggish 1%yr pace in 2027.

The Leading Index growth rate is still a touch firmer than
six months ago, the current pace of –0.14% comparing to a
–0.2% back in April, when the War in Iran was first impacting.
The component detail shows mixed moves over the
intervening period. Four components have started to exert
a bigger drag: total hours worked taking –0.2ppts off the
headline growth rate; with a further 0.15ppt drag coming
from the combined effect of softer commodity prices

(measured in AUD terms); a narrowing yield spread (mainly
reflecting the RBA tightening pushing up short term rates);
and rising job loss fears captured by the Westpac-Melbourne
Institute Unemployment Expectations Index. This has been
more than offset by a reduced (but still negative) drag from
the Westpac-Melbourne Institute Consumer Expectations
Index (adding +0.25ppts); and firmer reads on local equity
markets and dwelling approvals (adding +0.17ppts on a

combined basis.
Overall, the component mix shows labour market conditions
and tightening monetary policy have become more prominent
drags on momentum. While the Leading Index signal is not
overly weak the decline is clearly worth monitoring with the
detail suggesting there may be more weakening to come.
Leading Index moves back below trend
The Westpac-Melbourne Institute Leading Index of
Economic Activity is designed to assist in identifying
turning points in the economy. The index combines

variables that reflect different aspects of the economy
into a single index that generally produces a more reliable
cyclical indicator than any single component taken
individually. The Leading Index of Economic Activity
provides advance information on the state of the economy
and gives early warnings of cyclical turning points.
Matthew Hassan
Head of Australian Macro-Forecasting

Treasury yields paid minimal respect to the big moves in
equities through Monday. Yields, in fact, in the end, decided to
edge lower, mostly in a nod to the easing in the price of oil.
That will help take headline inﬂation rates oﬀ highs in
December CPI releases. But the upcoming US core PCE reading
will still head in the wrong direction. That needs to change

Padhraic Garvey, CFA and Michiel Tukker

### Page 2

The 10yr yield held in the mid-to-low 4.9% area, with, if anything, a
tendency to test lower in yield, which is the reverse of what would
be expected from a resumed risk-on day. The movement in the
price of oil had a more signiﬁcant correlation eﬀect, with Brent
managing to dip below $90/bbl. This is clearly helpful for headline
inﬂation rates down the line, and will show up in the December
inﬂation readings (reﬂecting November).
That said, the October personal consumption expenditure (PCE)

inﬂation reading is due on Wednesday, and it's not going to be
pretty. The good news is the market should already be anticipating
an acceleration in core PCE inﬂation to 3.2% year-on-year (YoY) (up
from 3.1% YoY). The bad news is this is still a high number, and it
continues to head in the wrong direction. Also, the fall in energy
prices will have a far less direct eﬀect on core PCE. Ultimately, it will,
of course, have some eﬀect, as, for example, airline fares can ease.

That apart, the bond market remains in a troubling environment
over the coming months. We suspect that, while the risk-on mode
seen on Monday may not be repeated immediately, the recent sell-
oﬀs in the AI space are, in fact, healthy over the medium term. They
can ultimately result in risk coming back on by the turn of the year.
On top of that, ﬁscal narratives stretching from Germany (defence)
to France (already bloated) to the US (untamed) are all adding to a

sense of heightened risks for core bonds. The big swing factor could
be inﬂation. But it's just too early to declare victory on that front
yet. So, we maintain a cautious bond view for the next few months.
Tuesday's events and market views
Markets will likely remain focused on the bounce back in AI
stocks. Eurozone data includes Spanish PPIs for October,
which, given the focus on second-round eﬀects, will be
watched. Other releases include Germany's Ifo survey results.

Consensus sees a slight improvement in the business climate
in November. From the US we have the Conference Board
consumer conﬁdence index for November. A slightly higher

### Page 2

The Week Ahead November 6, 2026 
                                                                                                                                         2

FORTHCOMING DATA 
RELEASES

Taiwan

Export growth is expected to ease to 
44.4% yoy in October from 60.9% in 
September. A higher comparison base 
makes exceptional export growth 
difficult to sustain from 4Q onwards. The 
global AI cycle is also expected to settle 
into a more normal pace into 2027, as 
higher interest rates lift capital costs 
and US mid-term election results 
heighten regulatory uncertainty. These 
factors underpin our forecast for 
Taiwan’s GDP growth to return to high 
single digits from 4Q onwards and into 
2027.

Malaysia

We expect Malaysia’s industrial 
production growth to rise to 5.5% yoy in 
September 2026 from 5.0% yoy in 
August. The uptick was likely driven by 
export-oriented manufacturing as 
strong artificial intelligence (AI)-related 
demand continued to support growth in 
electrical and electronics goods, 
alongside a smaller contraction in mining.

China

Export growth is expected to decelerate 
from 36.3% yoy in September to 28.1% in 
October. Both the official manufacturing 
PMI and new export orders sub-index fell 
below 50 after two months in expansion 
territory. High-frequency trade indicators 
also point to slowing momentum, with 
average daily deadweight tonnage handled 
at China’s 20 major ports falling from 7.2% 
yoy in September to -4.2% in October. 
Freight activity weakened as well, with 
growth in international cargo flights 
dropping from 3.6% yoy to -5.9% over the 
same period. On the price front, factory-gate 
and raw material price pressures moderated, 
with the main raw material purchasing price 
and producer price sub-indices declining 
from 60.8 and 54.0 to 56.8 and 51.7, 
respectively. Despite remaining above the 
50 threshold, the moderation suggests some 
easing in pipeline price pressures. 
Downstream consumer price pressures are 
also likely to remain subdued amid soft 
domestic demand. As a result, CPI inflation 
is expected to ease slightly from 0.9% yoy in 
September to 0.8% in October.

India

We expect headline inflation to rise to 6.0% 
yoy in October from 5.5% the month before, 
on higher food costs and a low base effect. 
High frequency data pointed to a further 
pick-up in sequential pressures in food 
segments, across cereals, edible oil, 
perishables (vegetables) etc. Non-food 
components should find relief from further 
moderation in precious metals and delay in 
passage of retail fuel price changes. Core 
inflation is nonetheless set to quicken to 4.5-
4.6% as services notch increases and as 
indirect tax effects in the base, fall out of the 
equation. With inflation expected to test 
past the higher end of the RBI target range, 
we expect a second successive rate hike in 
December. The decline in oil prices has also 
led to cooling of overnight OIS rates as 
concerns over aggressive rate hikes have 
eased. Goods trade deficit is likely to stay 
wide at above $30bn in Oct.

What is the biggest disconnect today
between what the market is pricing and
ING’s Global Outlook?
Our base case scenario is more dovish and benign than markets
are currently pricing in. We expect oil prices to fall further, easing
inﬂationary pressures and allowing central banks to stop hiking
rates after a ﬁnal rate hike in December.
Due to weaker household balance sheets, we see only limited pass-
through from higher energy prices to core inﬂation, with companies
instead having to squeeze proﬁt margins. In this scenario, major

central banks could even start considering rate cuts towards the

Is it normal for markets to be pricing in
virtually no rate cuts? Beyond 12 months,

How does that compare to historic rate hike
cycles?
Compared to today’s curve, in 2008 and early 2023 markets priced
in a much larger decline in rates beyond the near-term peak. The
current curve is much more similar in shape to the 2007 hiking

cycle.
The current curve is more similar to the
2007 hiking cycle than the one in 2023
Based on these prior hiking episodes, we foresee two conditions
that would warrant a more humped yield curve, i.e. a curve with a
lower terminal policy rate. The ﬁrst is a material slowdown in the
growth outlook. The most extreme version of this is the 2008 policy
error by the European Central Bank – we all know what happened
after the ECB decided to hike in 2008. So far, the growth outlook

remains robust, and we do not see this as a likely path for 2027.
The second trigger of a more humped-shaped yield curve would be
a decline in inﬂation risks. Once inﬂation peaked in 2023, markets

started repricing a lower neutral rate. In the near term, the curve still

Does Europe have a 'K-shaped' economy
like the US? And is there scope for
household savings to shield consumer
spending from higher energy prices in 2027?
Lower-income households generally suﬀer more from higher

energy prices than higher-income households, as they spend a
larger share of their income on energy and food. The energy shock
could therefore produce a more K-shaped consumption pattern.
However, Europe’s extensive social safety net provides important
protection for lower-income groups. Indeed, the consumer-
conﬁdence gap between the highest and lowest income quartiles
has not widened in 2026. Quite the opposite.
Since energy prices began rising, the savings rate has fallen,

cushioning the negative impact on consumption. However, with
households’ unemployment expectations increasing, it would be
overly optimistic to assume that the eurozone household savings
rate will continue to decline.
US growth has remained strong, but
deﬁcits are rising and a Democrat-run
Congress could put pressure on data centre
construction. Is the US heading towards a
debt crisis?
The US's ﬁscal position is poor. The government is currently
spending 4% of GDP on interest costs while running a primary

deﬁcit (tax revenue less departmental and programme spending) of
2.5% of GDP. The mid-term election results mean the primary deﬁcit
is pretty much locked in at 2.5% of GDP, with President Trump likely
vetoing any Democrat tax and spending policies approved in
Congress.

### Page 4

To keep the debt-to-GDP ratio stable, the economy needs to grow
faster than its debt pile to the tune of the primary deﬁcit. Today,
the US government is paying an average borrowing cost of 3.6%, so
nominal GDP needs to grow 6.1% per year to keep debt-to-GDP at
100% (6.1%-3.6%=2.5%). Amid rising government borrowing costs
and with nominal GDP growth closer to 5%, the debt-to-GDP ratio
looks set to climb.
But nothing is set in stone. For example, if AI leads to a

technological and productivity revolution that boosts growth and
tax revenue while lowering inﬂation and allowing interest costs to
fall, then the maths – and the outlook for the debt-to-GDP ratio –
changes dramatically.
Would the ECB lowering government
borrowing costs through bond purchases
encourage governments to borrow more,
worsening already-high debt levels? And is
it too soon for the ECB to ease policy, with
tools like QE?
Indeed, markets are inserting more disciplinary pressures on

governments that the European ﬁscal framework has failed to do.
This is why the ECB will be hesitant to restart bond purchases, at
least as long as governments are not subscribing to austerity
measures.
However, if the French ﬁscal crisis leads to contagion across the
eurozone while the rest of the eurozone sticks to the ﬁscal rules, the
ECB is likely to start its Transmission Protection Instrument to ring-
fence France and prevent contagion. The risk of starting any kind of

QE too early is indeed that it could create the wrong incentives for
ﬁscal policy.
The last US jobs report saw a small
uptick in wage growth. How likely is that to
accelerate, given tighter immigration policy

and labour shortages in some sectors, and
recent strength in the jobs data?
The best leading indicator for wage growth is the quits rate – the
proportion of workers quitting their jobs to move to a new position.
It peaked at 3% in 2022 as the post-Covid reopening fuelled intense
competition for workers. Companies responded by raising pay to
attract and retain staﬀ, pushing wage growth to 5.5%.
That situation has since reversed, with the quits rate down to 2%

and wage growth slowing sharply to just 3%. We are in a low-hire,
low-ﬁre economy with a lack of turnover in positions – few
companies are hiring, and few people are quitting, meaning
companies don't feel any real need to oﬀer big pay awards.
Some sectors are reporting shortages of skilled workers and there
will be pockets of strength, but until the quits rate moves
meaningfully higher, wage growth nationally will remain benign.
How will your outlook for the Federal

Reserve affect gold prices in 2027?
We expect the Fed to remain relatively cautious on rate cuts for
much of 2027, meaning monetary policy is unlikely to provide a
strong tailwind for gold early in the year. However, as expectations
for further easing build, lower yields and a potentially softer dollar
should become more supportive for bullion.
Combined with continued central bank buying, geopolitical
uncertainty and investor demand, we expect gold prices to be

supported in 2027, although periods of higher yields and dollar
strength could still trigger volatility.

No. Ed and Elias don’t believe the AI panic that has gripped investors will
topple the economy. Investors’ concerns aren’t unfounded, but they lack
the multiplier force to bring on a recession given three sturdy pillars of
economic resilience. Analysts’ earnings estimates are strong and rising;
investor confidence in those estimates has evaporated. … We’ve seen
this movie before, say Ed and Elias: Twice in the past (2018 and 2022),
when investor fears compressed valuation multiples, the selloff was a

correction in a bull market and a buying opportunity. We expect the same
this time. Our economic outlook remains unchanged: We assign a 70%
probability to continuation of the Roaring 2020s.
